Applicability of regulation on Internet Service Providers 
a) Inputs of the Stakeholders: 


I. 


Il. 


A few stakeholders commented that the amended Regulation | makes compliance voluntary for 
Internet Service Providers below the subscriber threshold of 0,000. This creates a category of 


consumers — typically in Tier-2/3 towns and rural areas, who have no legally enforceable 
grievance redressal mechanism, often relying on these smaller ISPs as their sole means of internet 
access. 


A few stakeholders suggested to extend the scope of these regulations to include app-based like 
OTT-based voice, video and messaging service providers. 


b) Analysis & Conclusion: 


I. 


Il. 


Il. 


Regulation 2 — Definitions: 


8) 


Definitions 


With regard to stakeholders’ concern about Service Provider’s compliance towards consumers 
in Tier 2/3 towns and rural areas, it is observed that many smaller ISPs operate in 
geographically remote, rural, semi-urban, Tier-2 and Tier-3 markets where they often provide 
last-mile connectivity and contribute significantly to broadband penetration and digital 
inclusion. Implementation of the mandatory framework would require such service providers 
to incur substantial operational, technological and administrative costs and may impose a 
compliance burden disproportionate to the scale of their operations. For smaller ISPs operating 
with a limited subscriber base and financial and human resources, such regulatory costs may 
adversely affect their commercial viability, discourage market participation and reduce 
competition, particularly in underserved and rural areas where smaller providers play an 
important role in extending broadband connectivity. 


The proposed amendment also aligns with the provisions of “The Standards of Quality of 
Service of Access (Wireline and Wireless) and Broadband (Wireline and Wireless) Service 
Regulations, 2024” wherein similar compliance provisions have been mentioned for ISPs. The 
Authority is therefore of the view that a proportionate regulatory approach is warranted. While 
consumer interests remain paramount, regulatory obligations must be calibrated having regard 
to the scale of operations and the likely consumer impact. 


With regards to suggestions for inclusion of app-based service providers, it is clarified that 
OTT services fall outside TRAI's regulatory jurisdiction. 


a) Inputs of the Stakeholders: 


I. 


Il. 


Certain stakeholders objected to the retention of proposed definitions of ‘Access Service’, 
‘Authorization’, ‘Survey’. 


Some stakeholders argued for removal of the definition of ‘Survey’ while others suggested TRAI 
to prescribe standardised survey formats, language, audits etc. 


78 THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : EXTRAORDINARY [PART III—SEc.4] 


Ill. A few stakeholders suggested to include “wireless media including Terrestrial and Satellite” in 
the definition of ‘Broadband’ and include ‘services availed by consumers’ in the definition of 
‘Service Query’ if it means the third-party apps being used by consumers. 


IV. Certain stakeholders suggested alignment of the definition of ‘Authorisation’ with Unified 
Licence regime. 


V. Some stakeholders suggested revisions to, and sought greater clarity in, specific definitions, 
including those relating to Access Service, Survey, Broadband, Service Query, Complaint 
Monitoring System (Requires Stronger Transparency Obligations), Grievance Redressal 
Mechanism (Should Include Accountability Principles) and Service Provider (Should Include 
Responsibility for Outsourced Agencies). 


b) Analysis: 


I. The Authority has examined the stakeholder comments pertaining to certain proposed definitions: 
l. “Access Service”: The definition is aligned with the relevant provisions of the Indian Telegraph 
Act,l885 and the Telecommunications Rules issued by the Department of Telecommunications 
(DoT) dated 23.06.2026, thereby ensuring consistency with the prevailing statutory and 
regulatory framework. 


i. Regarding the stakeholders’ comments for alignment of definition of “Authorization” with 
Unified License regime, the regulation is applicable for all service providers providing access 
services, and internet services. Further the definition of Service Provider is amended to include 
all entities having a license under the Indian Telegraph Act, 885 or an authorization under the 
Telecommunications Act, 2023 for providing services covered under sub-regulation (3) of 
regulation | 


ill. “Internet” and “Internet Services”: The definition is aligned with the relevant provisions of the 
Indian Telegraph Act, l885 and the Telecommunications Rules issued by the Department of 
Telecommunications (DoT) dated 23.06.2026, thereby ensuring consistency with the prevailing 
statutory and regulatory framework. 


iv. “Service Query” should encompass all services offered by service providers, not merely those 
currently availed by consumers. 


II.With regards to stakeholders’ suggestions on the definitions of ‘Complaint Monitoring System’, and 
“Grievance Redressal mechanism’, the Authority notes that as such the definitions are adequately clear. 


III. With respect to stakeholders’ suggestions for deletion of the definition of “Feedback Survey”, the Authority is 
of the view that the Feedback Survey prescribed under regulation l4A is an integral component of the 
grievance redressal framework for capturing consumer satisfaction upon resolution of complaints and 
appeals. Accordingly, the definition of “Feedback Survey” has been retained. 


Regulation 3 -Establishment of Complaint Centre: 
Sub-regulation (4) - 


9) Complaint centres Service in official language/languages of the states in the licensed service area 
a) Inputs of the Stakeholders: 


I. Certain stakeholders appreciated the amendments for strengthening consumers’ right to 
grievance redressal. 


Il. Some stakeholders opposed the multilingual support requirement, citing increased 
compliance burden and operational complexity, and contended that regulatory frameworks 
such as those of the RBI and the Department of Consumer Affairs provide greater flexibility 
in this regard. Some also argued for deletion of this proposal. 


[भाग ॥|--खण्ड 4] 


भारत का राजपत्र : असाधारण 79 


Il. 


Certain other stakeholders suggested that the language requirements for Complaint Centres 
and IVR systems may be reviewed to provide greater implementation flexibility, particularly 
with respect to the provision of services in the official language(s) of the licensed service 
area, in addition to Hindi and English. 


b) Analysis: 


I. 


Il. 


Il. 


IV. 


VI. 


VII. 


VII. 


IX. 


Sub-regulation (3)- 


One of the primary objectives of the proposed amendments is to remove ambiguities in the 
existing framework and ensure uniform implementation across all service providers. 


Under the extant regulation, the expression "local language" was susceptible to varying 
interpretations, resulting in inconsistent practices across service providers. 


Accordingly, the amendment proposes a clear and objective requirement that complaint 
handling facilities be made available in the official language(s) of the State(s) within the 
Licensed Service Area, in addition to Hindi and English, based on the option exercised by the 
consumer. 


This approach seeks to ensure consistency, eliminate ambiguity, and improve accessibility of 
grievance redressal mechanisms across service providers. 


Stakeholder comments regarding the requirement to provide consumer support services in the 
official language(s) of the State(s) within the Licensed Service Area, in addition to Hindi and 
English were examined. The Authority has noted the efforts are already being undertaken by 
service providers to facilitate consumer interactions in multiple languages. 


However, it is noted that a significant segment of the population, particularly consumers 
residing in remote and socio-economically disadvantaged areas, may not possess adequate 
proficiency in either Hindi or English in states where the official language is other than Hindi. 
For such consumers, access to grievance redressal mechanisms in the official language(s) of 
the concerned State is often essential for effective communication of service-related issues and 
for understanding the assistance being provided by the service provider. 


The Authority is of the view that language should not become a barrier to the exercise of 
consumer rights or access to grievance redressal mechanisms. The proposed amendment 
therefore seeks to strengthen inclusivity and accessibility by ensuring that consumers are able 
to interact with service providers in at least one of the other official language(s) of the State(s) 
within the Licensed Service Area, in addition to Hindi and English. 


The proposed substitution of the term “local language” with “official language(s) of the 
State(s) in the Licensed Service Area” provides greater regulatory clarity, removes ambiguity 
and promotes uniform implementation across the sector. 


It is also noted that some of the states/Union Territories have more than one official language, 
therefore considering the operational complexity as expressed by certain stakeholders, the 
Authority is of the view that complaint centres of the Service providers shall provide services 
in at least one of the official languages of the State(s) in the licensed service area, in addition to 
Hindi and English, based on the option exercised by the consumer. 


20) Round-the-clock operation of complaint centres 


a) Inputs of the Stakeholders: 


I. 


Certain stakeholders supported the proposed provision, noting that it is aligned with the essential 
and continuous nature of telecom services and the need for uninterrupted consumer grievance 
redressal and maintained that operational costs are an inherent responsibility of service providers 
and cannot justify denial or dilution of consumer services. 


80 


THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : EXTRAORDINARY [PART III—SEc.4] 


Il. 


Il. 


IV. 


Some stakeholders opposed the 24x7 requirements citing disproportionate costs and limited 
benefit. 


Some other stakeholders expressed the view that the proposed operational model should be 
assessed in light of implementation feasibility and the expected consumer benefits. They 
suggested extending the service support from 0600 hours onwards, at best, in a hybrid mode at 
the choice of TSPs. 


Some stakeholders supported round-the-clock operations of Complaint Centres. 


b) Analysis: 


Il. 


Il. 


IV. 


VI. 


VII. 


It is noted that telecommunications is now an essential service that facilitates access to a wide 
range of critical activities, including banking, digital payments, healthcare, education, emergency 
communications etc. Consequently, disruptions in telecommunication services may occur at any 
time and can significantly affect consumers, irrespective of the hour at which such disruptions 
arise. Such a requirement has also been acknowledged by certain service providers while stating 
that a reasonable number of calls start coming 6:00 am onwards. 


The Authority is of the view that consumers should have continuous access to channels for 
reporting service-related issues and seeking assistance. Restricting the operational hours of 
Complaint Centres may result in situations where consumers experiencing service disruptions 
during late-night or early-morning hours are unable to register complaints or obtain support in a 
timely manner. 


The availability of human assistance enhances consumers’ perception that their grievances are 
being acknowledged and actively addressed, thereby improving consumer confidence, 
satisfaction, and trust in the service provider’s grievance redressal mechanism. The amendment 
states that the complaint centre shall provide efficient consumer assistance through human 
representatives or automated systems or AI agents or other consumer-centric technological 
solutions, or a combination thereof, round the clock. However, human representative-assisted 
support shall be made available for a minimum of sixteen hours daily, on all days of the week. 


The proposed requirement therefore seeks to ensure that all consumers, irrespective of their work 
schedules, geographical location or personal circumstances, have access to grievance registration 
facilities whenever required. 


The Authority further notes that the requirement for round-the-clock availability of Complaint 
Centres does not necessarily imply deployment of the same level of resources 24X7. Consumer 
interaction volumes during late-night hours are generally lower than those during peak business 
hours, and service providers may deploy resources in a manner commensurate with expected 
demand while ensuring continuous accessibility of complaint registration and support 
mechanisms. In practice, complaints received during such periods are often limited to urgent 
service-related issues, while detailed resolution activities may continue during normal operational 
hours. 


It is observed that a number of essential and consumer-facing services across sectors have 
increasingly adopted 24x7 support frameworks in recognition of evolving consumer expectations 
and the continuous nature of digital service delivery. Given the critical role of 
telecommunications as an enabling infrastructure for such services, it is appropriate that 
consumers are provided uninterrupted access to complaint registration and support facilities. 


The Authority is of the view that Complaint Centre should operate round the clock on all days of 
the week and shall provide efficient consumer assistance through human representatives or 
automated systems or AI agents or other customer-centric technological solutions, or a 


[भाग ॥|--खण्ड 4] भारत का राजपत्र : असाधारण 8 


combination thereof, round the clock. Access to human representatives should be available for at 
least 6 hours daily. During the remaining hours, consumer support may be provided through 
automated and other consumer-centric technological solutions. 


VII. Amendment provides that the consumer shall get a notification that he is communicating with an 
AI Agent as and when such a situation occurs. Automated systems or AI agents or other 
consumer-centric technological solutions deployed at Complaint Centre shall operate with 
appropriate human oversight. 


Sub regulation (9), (J0) and (J]) - 
2) Operation of IVRS, Web _portal/websites, Mobile application & new age consumer centric solutions 
(Chatbots, AI Agents etc). 


a) Inputs of the Stakeholders: 


I. Certain stakeholders supported a common complaint framework, observing that service providers 
retain flexibility in interface design while ensuring consistent grievance procedures. They endorse 
modernisation of the grievance mechanism through web-portal and mobile-application channels for 
complaint/appeal registration. 


II. Some stakeholders opposed any modification to the existing [VRS structure, contending that the 
current framework adequately facilitates consumer grievance redressal. It argued that introducing 
an ‘Appeals’ option at level 2 will increase confusion among the consumers, creating unnecessary 
complexity and procedural overlaps. 


Il. Some stakeholders suggested that ‘Acknowledgement’ should also include recording of conversations 
for consumer protection. 


IV. Some suggested that complaints be closed only after consumer confirmation through OTP, SMS, 
email or mobile application; otherwise, they should be automatically escalated for review. 


V. Some other stakeholders advocated mandatory access to human assistance at every IVRS level instead 
of only the third level. 


VI. Some further suggested a dedicated ‘Appeals’ option at second/third options for increased 
accessibility. 


VII. One stakeholder proposed two modifications to the IVR flow: (l) at the second level add an option 
"Giv) Not Sure/Others" that auto-routes to a call-back or human contact; (2) enumerate and 
standardise the third-level categories across the industry. 


VIN. Another stakeholder has recommended mandatory human fallback under sub-regulation . 


IX. Some stakeholders sought dispensing with of the mandatory toll-free Consumer Care Number under 
regulation 3(7), arguing that with unlimited voice bundled into data plans, calls are effectively free 
(making the original rationale redundant), and thus, toll-free infrastructure imposes disproportionate 
cost/duplication on lean-infrastructure ISPs — seeking parity with TRAI's similar rationalization 
for Cable TV/DPOs. 


b) Analysis: 


I. The Authority has examined the stakeholders’ suggestions for retention of the existing IVRS 
structure and notes that a key shortcoming in the existing grievance redressal framework was the 
lack of uniformity in the structure and operation of Interactive Voice Response Systems (IVRS) 
deployed by different service providers. In many instances, consumers were required to navigate 
multiple layers of menus before reaching the appropriate grievance category. Further, options 
relating to appeals were often not distinctly identified and, in certain cases, were embedded 


82 


THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : EXTRAORDINARY [PART III—SEc.4] 


Il. 


Il. 


IV. 


VI. 


VII. 


VII. 


IX. 


XI. 


within broader categories such as service requests or general queries, resulting in confusion and 
inconvenience to consumers. 


To address these concerns, the proposed amendment introduces a standardized architecture 
throughout all modes [as per sub-regulation 9 (IVRS, AI agents), sub-regulation!0 (web portal or 
mobile application, chatbots, AI agents) and sub-regulationl] (new-age solutions including 
messaging platforms such as WhatsApp) of regulation 3] applicable across service providers. 
Under the revised framework, the first stage is concerned with language selection. 


The second stage is required to clearly present three distinct categories: 
a. Complaints; 
b. Appeals; and 
c. Service Requests/Queries. 


The third stage is concerned with accessing context-specific sub-menu for each category of 
complaint, appeal, service request or service queries. 


The Authority is of the view that this clear categorisation will significantly improve consumer 
awareness regarding available grievance redressal channels and ensure that appeals are explicitly 
identifiable rather than being subsumed within generic query or consumer service options. 


The proposed amendment also mandates that the consumers shall be provided with: 
a. an option to connect with a human consumer care representative; and 
b. an option to avail a call back facility while waiting in the queue to connect with a human 
representative. 


These provisions have been specifically incorporated to ensure that consumers are not compelled 
to remain within automated workflows and can seek human intervention whenever required. 


Regarding the stakeholders’ suggestions seeking mandatory human fallback at every level, the 
Authority notes that the availability of a human-assisted channel at the third stage strikes an 
appropriate balance between operational efficiency achieved through automation and the need for 
effective consumer support. 


It is observed that consumers often experience inconvenience and loss of time due to the 
placement of information relating to existing plans, offers, and promotional services at the 
commencement of customer care interactions. Accordingly, the proposed framework seeks to 
streamline the grievance redressal process by requiring service providers to present consumers 
with clearly identifiable grievance-related options at the initial stages of the IVRS while 
information relating to plans, products, and services may continue to be made available under a 
dedicated category without impeding access to complaint registration, appeals, or service request 
mechanisms. This is expected to reduce call handling complexity and enable consumers to access 
the desired grievance redressal channel more efficiently. 


The Authority has also examined concerns expressed by certain Service Providers regarding the 
possibility of consumers inadvertently selecting the “Appeals” option at the second stage of the 
IVRS without having first registered a complaint. In this regard, the Authority is of the view that 
there should be no presumption against the wisdom of consumers in distinguishing among 
complaints, appeals and service requests/queries, particularly when these are clearly delineated 
within the grievance redressal framework. 


The amendment provides that the Complaint Centre shall register appeals round the clock and 
shall provide assistance through a human representative for registration of appeals during the 
hours when human representatives are available at the Complaint Centre. Such a mechanism 
would ensure that consumers are guided to the correct grievance redressal channel while 
preventing procedural confusion and maintaining the integrity of the complaint and appeal 
handling process. Appeals can also be registered round-the-clock through app or web portal. 


[भाग ॥|--खण्ड 4] 


भारत का राजपत्र : असाधारण 83 


XI. 


XII. 


XIV. 


XV. 


With regard to concerns relating to digitally excluded consumers, while the amendments 
recognize evolving consumer preferences by enabling complaint registration through websites, 
mobile applications, chatbots and Al-enabled platforms, the conventional communication 
channels including helpline numbers and other complaint registration mechanism through IVRS, 
Complaint Centres and Consumer Care Numbers continue to remain available. The proposed 
digital channels are intended to supplement, and not replace, existing consumer support 
arrangements. 


Some stakeholders suggested mandating OTP/ SMS-based consumer confirmation prior to 
closure of complaints. The Authority is of the view that the Feedback Survey prescribed under 
regulation l4A adequately captures consumer satisfaction following complaint resolution. 


With regard to stakeholders’ suggestions for setting up of National Consumer Helpline (NCH), 
the Authority notes that this issue is outside the scope of the present regulation. Notably, 
consumers already have access to the National Consumer Helpline (NCH), which functions as a 
centralized platform for consumer grievance assistance across sectors, including 
telecommunications. Furthermore, the primary responsibility for addressing and resolving 
consumer complaints rests with the concerned service provider. The grievance redressal 
framework prescribed under these regulations is designed to strengthen the accountability of 
service providers. 


The Authority has reviewed the comments seeking dispensation of the mandatory toll-free 
Consumer Care Number under regulation 3(7) and has decided that regulation to be modified as 
“The “Consumer Care Number” shall not be chargeable to consumers. 


Sub-regulation (2) - 
22) Accessibility for Persons with disability (PwD) 


a) 


b) 


Il. 


IV. 


Il. 


Il. 


Inputs of the Stakeholders: 


. Introduction of provision for Persons with disability (PwDs) has been well received by stakeholders. 


. Certain stakeholders have proposed that they have already deployed appropriate processes, in line 


with the DoT instructions dated 3].07.2025, and addition of this provision is mere duplication of 
existing practices already implemented by service providers. 


One of the stakeholders suggested to use word ‘shall’ in place of ‘should’. 


Some stakeholders recommended extending similar safeguards to all vulnerable consumers and 
mandating non-digital complaint channels. 


Analysis: 


The Authority has noted the stakeholder comments indicating that dedicated support mechanisms for 
Persons with Disabilities (PwDs) are already being provided by service providers pursuant to the 
instructions issued by the Department of Telecommunications (DoT) dated 3] .07.2025. 


However, the Authority notes that the proposed provisions under regulation 3 are not intended to 
duplicate existing requirements but rather to ensure that such accessibility measures continue to 
remain an integral part of the consumer grievance redressal framework and are uniformly available 
across all service providers. Existing systems, processes and dedicated facilities established pursuant 
to the DoT instructions may continue to be utilised, with such modifications and enhancements as 
may be necessary to comply with the requirements of these regulations. 


It is also noted that the DoT instructions dated 3].07.2025 were issued in the context of provisioning 
of telecommunication services to PwDs, including accessibility of Know Your Customer (KYC) 
processes, onboarding procedures and related support mechanisms. The instructions also provide for 
accessibility-related grievance handling and dedicated assistance facilities. In pursuance to the DoT 


84 THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : EXTRAORDINARY [PART III—SEc.4] 


direction, this provision will ensure that the direction becomes a regulatory binding upon all service 
providers, while providing more accessibility and dedicated assistance facilities for PwDs. 


IV. To focus upon a comprehensive framework for registration, handling and resolution of telecom 
consumer complaints and appeals, the proposed provisions relating to special desks, dedicated 
helplines, routing of calls from PwD consumers, and accessibility features in consumer-facing 
grievance redressal channels seek to strengthen and integrate accessibility requirements within the 
broader consumer protection and complaint handling framework established under these regulations. 


V. The Authority is of the view that such provisions complement the existing DoT directions and ensure 
that PwD consumers receive appropriate assistance not only during service acquisition but also 
throughout the lifecycle of their interaction with the service provider. Moreover, while the DoT 
instructions are intended for implementation by service providers, these regulations remain publicly 
accessible to all consumers. 


VI. Some stakeholders have highlighted that regulation 3(/2) introduces ICT accessibility provisions for 
Persons with Disabilities; however, the regulation uses the word "should" in several PwD-related 
provisions, while identical provisions for general consumers use "shall". Accordingly, it has been 
decided to replace the word with “ shall”. 


Regulation 5 - Publication of Information: 
Sub-regulation (l) and (4) 
23) Publication of Information on newspapers, Provision of consumer corner on their websites. 


a) Inputs of the Stakeholders: 


I. Some stakeholders welcomed this amendment of mandatory consumer corner. They stated that 
consumers often face difficulties even in filing complaints, and at present consumers are often expected 
to resolve technical issues on their own before their complaints are entertained by Service Providers. 
Therefore, Service Providers should provide proper training and guidance to consumers at the time of 
giving telecom connections. 


Il. Some stakeholders stated that a separate ‘Consumer Comer’ would overlap with TRAI’s earlier 
direction dated |2.03.2026 and lead to duplication 


Il. Some other stakeholders opposed the requirement for newspaper publications every six months, 
contending that print media has largely become obsolete, consumers increasingly rely on digital 
platforms for information, and the proposed requirement would impose avoidable compliance and 
publication costs on service providers. 


IV. Certain stakeholders highlighted that the Authority had already set a precedent by removing the 
mandate for newspaper publication of tariff information vide Direction dated 03.04.209, recognising 
that TSPs provide all information on their websites and mobile applications. 


V. Some stakeholders proposed a half-yearly or monthly SMS broadcast as an alternative to publication in 
newspapers. 
VI. Some suggested that the Consumer Corner should include a 'Know Your Rights' section containing 


complaint filing rights, appeal rights, tariff rights, VAS deactivation rights, refund rules, network 
outage rights, spam complaint rights, compensation provisions, and QoS benchmarks. Consumer 
protection becomes ineffective if rights are unknown. The Consumer Corer should function not 
merely as a complaint page, but as a digital consumer rights charter. 


b) Analysis: 
I. The Authority has examined the stakeholder suggestions seeking discontinuation of newspaper-based 


publication requirements on the ground that newspapers have become obsolete and that information 
dissemination may be undertaken exclusively through digital platforms.

Il. 


Il. 


IV. 


VI. 


VIL. 


VII. 


IX. 


In this regard, the Authority recognizes that an effective grievance redressal framework depends not 
only upon the existence of complaint handling mechanisms but also upon consumers being adequately 
informed about their rights, available remedies, escalation channels, and the procedures to be followed 
for seeking redressal. The proposed amendments to regulation 5 seek to address these concerns by 
strengthening transparency, accessibility and consumer awareness through enhanced disclosure 
requirements. 


The amendment requiring publication of prescribed consumer information in Hindi, English and at 
least one of the official language(s) of the State(s) within the Licensed Service Area is intended to 
ensure that consumers can easily access and understand information relating to grievance redressal 
irrespective of linguistic preferences. 


It is recognized that consumer information dissemination practices have evolved significantly with the 
increasing adoption of digital communication channels. However, it is also true that a substantial 
section of the population continues to rely upon newspapers, whether in printed or digital form, as an 
important and credible source of information. This is particularly relevant for consumers residing in 
rural and semi-urban areas, senior citizens, and consumers who may not regularly access service 
providers’ digital platforms. 


Thus, the Authority is of the view that Service Providers need not publish a separate standalone 
advertisement for this purpose. Instead, the prescribed information may be carried below their regular 
commercial newspaper advertisements at least once every six months. 


The suggestion that Service Providers may disseminate Customer Care numbers and other mandatory 
consumer information through SMS broadcasts to all subscribers at least once every six months, in 
addition to the prescribed publication requirements is also agreed to. 


The Authority has further reviewed the comments regarding removal of such mandate of newspaper 
publication under the direction issued on 03.04.20l9 concerning tariff information. It is noted that the 
direction was issued in a distinct regulatory context. Tariff-related information is dynamic in nature 
and subject to frequent revisions, making newspaper publication an inefficient mode for routine 
dissemination. The present provision, however, pertains to consumer awareness and grievance 
redressal information, which is relatively static in nature and required to be published only at periodic 
intervals. 


The Authority is therefore of the view that the requirement for publication of such information through 
newspapers continues to serve an important consumer awareness objective by ensuring wider outreach 
across diverse consumer segments. Further, the prescribed publication frequency is limited and does 
not impose a recurring compliance burden comparable to information that undergoes frequent 
changes. Accordingly, the Authority considers that retaining newspaper publication requirements 
alongside digital dissemination channels is appropriate for ensuring maximum consumer awareness 
and inclusivity. It is suggested that all requisite publications be consolidated and issued simultaneously 
to optimize costs. 


With respect to comments highlighting overlap of proposed ‘Consumer Corner’ with TRAI’s earlier 
direction dated 2.03.2026, the amendment mandates that every service provider shall prominently 
display a dedicated section titled “Customer Care” on the home page of their website, containing 
statistics and details relating to - 

a) details of Complaint Centre; 

b) details of Appellate Authority; 

c) report of consumer feedback surveys; 

d) quarterly performance reports of redressal of complaints and appeals. 
The Authority notes that service providers have already undertaken implementation measures pursuant 
to the Direction dated 2.03.2026. 


86 


THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : EXTRAORDINARY [PART III—SEc.4] 


XL. 


XI. 


XII. 


XIV. 


The proposed amendment seeks to reinforce the existing direction and not replace it. Accordingly, the 
title of the dedicated tab or link on the homepage may continue to be displayed as “Customer Care” in 
accordance with the Direction dated 2.03.2026. 


However, while retaining the existing nomenclature for the sake of continuity and consumer 
familiarity, service providers shall incorporate the additional requirements prescribed under the 
amended regulations, including enhanced disclosure obligations, complaint and appeal facilitation 
mechanisms, consumer information requirements and other measures intended to improve consumer 
awareness, accessibility and overall grievance redressal experience. 


Further, the stakeholders have suggested a ‘Know Your Rights’ section under ‘ “Customer Care’. The 
information gap, if any, is addressed through the Telecom Consumers Charter prescribed under 
regulation 7, which is required to be prominently made available at the offices of the service 
provider, Complaint Centres, sales outlets, websites, and mobile applications and a copy of the 
Telecom Consumer charter or its abridged version shall be provided to each consumer at the time of 
subscription for service, thereby ensuring easy access to consumer-related information and grievance 
redressal procedures. 


The amendments are expected to improve transparency and enable consumers to more effectively 
avail themselves of the grievance redressal mechanisms established under these regulations as public 
disclosure of such information will facilitate informed decision-making and strengthen accountability 
of service providers through greater transparency. 


Regulation 6 - Establishment of Complaint Monitoring System: 
Sub-regulation (), (2), (3) and (4) 
24) Dissemination of information regarding complaint monitoring system. 


a) Inputs of the Stakeholders: 


I. 


Il. 


Il. 


Some stakeholders requested that this sub-regulation ensuring, that consumers receive regular updates 
regarding complaint status, actions taken, and projected resolution timelines through the application 
interface, as well as email and SMS, requires to be deleted as most service providers are already 
practising this mechanism. Therefore, there is no need for regulatory intervention on this aspect, as it 
will create unnecessary compliance burden. 


One Industry Association opposed the requirement for newspaper publications every 6 months, 
contending that print media has largely become obsolete, consumers increasingly rely on digital 
platforms for information, and the proposed requirement would impose avoidable compliance and 
publication costs on service providers. Some other stakeholders also hold a similar opinion and 
propose a sunset clause of one year on newspaper publications. 


One stakeholder commented that TRAI should launch an app for automatic forward of queries and 
help consumers track real time progress. The proposed application should automatically capture and 
transmit the details like Complainant Name, Mobile Number, geo coordinates, timestamp, signal 
parameters and coverage status, while lodging a complaint. 


b) Analysis: 


I. 


Il. 


The proposed amendments seek to strengthen the Complaint Monitoring System by broadening its 
scope to accommodate contemporary digital architectures and communication channels, while 
enhancing transparency, accessibility, and consumer participation throughout the complaint resolution 
process. 


Complaint Monitoring System, as defined in regulation 2(ha), means any World Wide Web-based 
system using client server architecture or other similar architecture, established under sub-regulation 
(4) of regulation 6, to register, track or monitor and manage complaints, appeals, service requests and 
service queries of the consumer. The amendment replacing the expression “Web based Complaint 
Monitoring System” with “Complaint Monitoring System” is intended to provide technological 


[भाग ॥|--खण्ड 4] भारत का राजपत्र : असाधारण 87 


flexibility and ensure that the regulatory framework remains adaptable to future developments in 
digital service delivery. This approach enables service providers to adopt modern platforms and 
architectures, including mobile application-based interfaces, without requiring frequent regulatory 
modifications. 


Il. The Authority has also proposed enhanced disclosure requirements to improve consumer awareness 
regarding the availability and use of the Complaint Monitoring System. 


IV. The Authority has reviewed the requirement of newspaper publication and has decided that henceforth 
Service Providers need not publish a separate standalone advertisement for this purpose. Instead, the 
prescribed information shall be carried below their regular commercial newspaper advertisements at 
least once every six months. 


V. The Authority also agreed with the suggestion that Service Providers shall disseminate Consumer 
Care numbers and other mandatory consumer information through SMS broadcasts to all subscribers 
at least once every six months, in addition to the prescribed publication requirements. 


VI. The Authority notes that service providers may employ different technological platforms and 
operational processes for communicating with consumers. However, the lack of periodic updates 
often results in consumer uncertainty, repeated follow-up requests and dissatisfaction with the 
grievance handling process, even where the underlying complaint is being actively addressed. 


VII. The Authority further notes that one of its statutory objectives is to protect the interests of consumers 
of telecommunication services and to ensure that telecommunication services are provided in a 
manner that promotes consumer welfare and satisfaction. In furtherance of this mandate, it is 
necessary to ensure that consumers remain adequately informed regarding the progress, expected 
resolution timelines and final outcome of their complaints and appeals. 


VII. The proposed amendment therefore seeks to establish a minimum and uniform standard of consumer 
communication across all service providers. Such a framework enhances transparency, reduces 
information asymmetry and enables consumers to track the status of their grievances without the need 
for repeated interactions with customer support channels. 


IX. The Authority therefore considers it appropriate to retain the proposed amendment in the interest of 
consumer convenience, confidence and satisfaction. 


Xx. With regard to stakeholder suggestions regarding development of a standardised mobile application 
framework, regulation 3(l0) already prescribes a structured framework for complaint registration, 
tracking and status monitoring, thereby addressing the essential consumer-facing requirements at this 
stage. Further, the Authority has developed consumer-centric digital platforms such as TRAIT 
MySpeed and TRAI DND, which facilitate network quality monitoring and reporting of unsolicited 
commercial communications, respectively. Accordingly, the Authority does not consider additional 
standardisation requirements necessary at present.

Sub-regulation (), (2) and (3)

complaints. 


a) Inputs of the Stakeholders: 


I. Some stakeholders stated the fact that sometimes service providers administratively close the 
complaint without consumer confirmation, which undermines trust in grievance systems. 
Therefore, the amendment in sub-regulation (3) is required to improve communication 
regarding actions taken on complaints. 


II. A few stakeholders agreed with the proposed six-month retention period for complaint records 
but argued that a three-month period would be adequate.

conversations for consumer protection. The stakeholder also supported requirement of six-

V. Some other stakeholders opposed the TRAI’s provision of increasing the retention limit of

b) Analysis: 


I. 


Il. 


Il. 


IV. 


reference numbers at the time of registration of complaints through all channels, including 
apps, websites, call centres, and chatbot platforms. 


The Authority notes the stakeholders’ suggestions for reducing the record retention period for 
complaints to three months to minimise the retention burden and operational costs. A minimum 
retention period of six months is necessary to facilitate audits and inspections. Accordingly, to 
strike a balance between data adequacy and compliance burden, the Authority is of the view that 
the six-month period is appropriate and proportional. 


There have been concerns regarding delays in receipt of complaint reference numbers, inadequate 
communication regarding actions taken on complaints, and instances where complaints were 
perceived to have been closed without satisfactory resolution from the consumer's perspective. 


The existing and proposed framework already envisages generation and communication of a 
unique docket number for every complaint registered by a consumer. The proposed amendments 
further strengthen this framework by mandating communication of complaint-related information 
and actions taken through multiple digital channels, including SMS and email under regulation 
7(3), thereby ensuring that consumers receive timely and documented updates regarding the status 
of their complaints. 


With regard to stakeholders’ comments highlighting lengthy and confusing complaint registration 
procedures, under the proposed amendments, service providers shall be required to communicate, 
through SMS as well as email the details of the action taken on the complaint along with the 
procedure for preferring an appeal before the Appellate Authority. 


Further, with respect to the stakeholders’ concerns on the issue of automated “issue resolved” 
messages despite unresolved grievances, the Authority has proposed amendments to strengthen the 
appellate mechanism by streamlining appeal procedures and reducing delays in escalation and 
resolution. These measures are intended to provide consumers with an effective remedy in cases 
where they are not satisfied with the redressal provided at the complaint handling stage.

VI. With respect to stakeholders’ suggestions for seeking confirmation before complaint closures, it is 
observed that the amendments introduce a structured mechanism for conducting online consumer 
surveys, under regulation |4A, upon closure or resolution of complaints and appeals. Such survey- 
based feedback mechanism is expected to provide valuable regulatory insights regarding consumer 
experience, facilitate identification of systemic issues, and support evidence-based regulatory 
interventions aimed at improving grievance redressal outcomes. 


VII. Regarding the comments seeking that consumers shall be permitted to reopen complaints within 
seven days where the issue persists or re-occurs, it is clarified that the option for filing an Appeal 
has been provided for such cases. 


Regulation 8 - Time limit for redressal of complaints or addressing service requests of consumers: 
Sub-regulation (l) and (2) 


26) Time limits. 


a) Inputs of the Stakeholders: 


I. 


Il. 


Il. 


IV. 


VI. 


VII. 


VII. 


Certain stakeholders supported the proposed timelines and escalation mechanisms, noting that they would 
enhance consumer protection, improve service responsiveness, and strengthen accountability in grievance 
redressal. 


Some stakeholders supported the provision of maximum three days for complaints and service requests 
where no specific timelines are prescribed. 


Some stakeholders pointed out that the three-day time period for complaints where no specific period is 
mentioned is unduly restrictive and operationally impractical. 


Some stakeholders have recommended harmonisation of the proposed complaint resolution timelines and 
procedures with existing regulatory frameworks to ensure consistency and avoid overlapping compliance 
requirements. 


One stakeholder has stated that the current residual timeline of three days for complaints where no specific 
period is prescribed is unduly restrictive and operationally impractical. 


Certain stakeholders suggested prescribing differentiated and expedited resolution timelines for critical or 
high-impact complaints (such as network outages, SIM-related fraud, financial fraud, and essential 
broadband disruptions), along with appropriate consumer safeguards in case of delays. 


Several stakeholders have also suggested that the complaint timelines and procedures under the Draft 
Amendment should be harmonized with the Quality-of-Service Regulations, the Telecom Commercial 
Communications Customer Preference Regulations, 20l8 and the Telecom Consumers Protection 
Regulations, 20!2 to avoid overlapping, inconsistent or conflicting obligations. 


Some stakeholders suggested that TRAI may consider prescribing strict timelines for processing refunds 
along with automatic compensation or interest for delays beyond the prescribed period and that the refund 
should be processed within 3 days. 


b) Analysis: 


I. 


With regard to stakeholders’ comments to ensure consistency among existing regulatory frameworks, the 
Authority notes that timely redressal of consumer grievances is one of the fundamental objectives of an 
effective consumer protection framework. In the absence of clearly defined timelines, consumers may face 
uncertainty regarding the status of their complaints and service requests, while service providers may adopt 
varying practices in handling grievances, resulting in inconsistency in consumer experience and difficulties in 
regulatory oversight. 


90 


THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : EXTRAORDINARY [PART III—SEc.4] 


Il. 


Il. 


IV. 


VI. 


VII. 


The proposed amendment seeks to establish a harmonized and consistent framework by requiring service 
providers to comply with the timelines already prescribed under various regulations issued by the Authority, 
including the Standards of Quality of Service of Access (Wireline and Wireless) and Broadband (Wireline and 
Wireless) Services Regulations, 2024, the Telecom Commercial Communication Customer Preference 
Regulations, 208, and the Telecom Consumers Protection Regulations, 202. 


The Authority is of the view that cross-referencing the timelines prescribed under these regulations serves 
multiple regulatory objectives. 


a. Firstly, it ensures consistency and uniformity across the regulatory framework. 

b. Secondly, it avoids duplication of provisions and potential interpretational conflicts arising from 
different timelines for similar categories of complaints. 

c. Thirdly, it provides greater clarity and certainty to both consumers and service providers regarding 
applicable obligations. 


Some stakeholders have also stated that the 3-day time period would be operationally impractical for certain 
kinds of complaints. The Authority notes that a period of three days has been prescribed keeping in view the 
need to balance consumer interests with operational feasibility for service providers. Regulation 8(2) requires 
that complaints for which no specific timeline has been prescribed be appropriately “addressed” within three 
days. In case resolution is not possible within that period; the consumers should receive a timely response 
indicating the anticipated resolution timeline, reasons for delay, and the parties involved, thereby promoting 
transparency and consumer-centric grievance handling. 


The Authority notes that suggestions relating to SIM deactivation, OTP delivery failures and digital payment 
disruptions shall be handled through the framework prescribed under these regulations. However, fraud-related 
issues lie beyond the scope of these regulations. 


With regard to suggestions for prescribing refund timelines, the Authority notes that timelines relating to 
refund of deposits are governed under The Standards of Quality of Service of Access (Wireline and Wireless) 
and Broadband (Wireline and Wireless) Service Regulations 2024 (06 of 2024). The proposed amendments do 
not alter the timelines prescribed under The Standards of Quality of Service of Access (Wireline and Wireless) 
and Broadband (Wireline and Wireless) Service Regulations 2024 (06 of 2024) Regulations, the Telecom 
Commercial Communication Customer Preference Regulations, 20l8 (06 of 20l8), Telecom Consumers 
Protection Regulations 20l2 (02 of 202). 


With regard to suggestions for provision of consumer compensation, the Authority highlights that such matters 
amount to dealing with individual complaint issues which is not covered in its regulatory mandate. 


Regulation 9 - Appeal to Appellate Authority: 
Sub-regulation (2) and (3) 


27) 


Time limit for filing of appeals. 
a) Inputs of the Stakeholders: 


I. Certain stakeholders supported the proposed amendment to expedite the process, ensure quicker 
escalation, and promote timely resolution. They supported a structured, tiered grievance redressal 
mechanism that resolves consumer disputes internally before they reach the courts. 


Il. Some stakeholders recommended retaining the existing 30-day timeline for filing an appeal instead of 
the proposed |5-day period, stating that it provides consumers with adequate time to assess the 
resolution of their complaints before preferring an appeal. 


Il. Some stakeholders suggested discontinuing the provision for filing appeals through post, 
recommending that the appeal mechanism be aligned with the objective of promoting digital modes of 
consumer grievance redressal. 


IV. One stakeholder stated that 'Complaint through’ may be replaced by 'IN-PERSON'. Some suggested a 
longer appeal filing period of three months. 


[भाग ॥|--खण्ड 4] 


भारत का राजपत्र : असाधारण 94 


VI. 


VII. 


Some stakeholders supported reduction of appeal timeline but recommended further reduction to 0 
days and mandatory proactive communication to consumer within 5 days if delay is anticipated while 
others suggested permitting delayed appeals up to 45 days, subject to sufficient cause. 


Some stakeholders also recommended a TRAI portal-based appeal registration mechanism to prevent 
service providers from suppressing or ignoring consumer appeals and suggested that TRAI receive 
and forward such appeals to service providers to ensure greater neutrality. 


Some stakeholders suggested that every complaint closure communication should clearly inform 
consumers of their right to appeal, the appeal process, and the applicable timeline for filing an appeal, 
to improve consumer awareness and facilitate informed exercise of appellate rights. One of them 
recommended limiting the modes of filing appeals to digital and in-person channels by removing the 
option of filing appeals through post/courier, with the objective of aligning the appeal mechanism 
with a digitally enabled grievance redressal framework. 


b) Analysis: 


I. 


Il. 


Il. 


IV. 


VI. 


VIL. 


With respect to stakeholders’ comments regarding extension/ reduction of timelines, the Authority 
notes that the appellate process is intended to provide an expeditious review of complaints that have 
either not been resolved within the prescribed timelines or where the consumer is dissatisfied with the 
redressal provided by the Complaint Centre. 


In such cases, a prolonged interval between disposal of the complaint and initiation of the appeal may 
unnecessarily delay the commencement of appellate proceedings and the eventual resolution of the 
grievance. 


The time limit for filing an appeal following disposal of a complaint has been reduced from 30 days to 
I5 days as consumers would normally prefer appeal as soon as possible whereas a longer time 
allowed unduly increases compliance requirements. The proposed amendment of reducing the appeal 
filing period from thirty (30) days to fifteen ((5) days is aimed at expediting the overall grievance 
redressal cycle and ensuring that appeals are examined while the facts, records, and circumstances of 
the complaint remain current and readily available. 


Some stakeholders have suggested the extension of appeal filing timeline. The Authority is of the 
view that extending the appeal filing period would proportionately increase the overall time required 
for completion of the grievance redressal process and may adversely impact timely resolution of 
complaints across the sector. 


Further, stakeholders have expressed their concerns to improve consumer awareness and facilitate 
informed exercise of appellate rights. The proposed amendments to Regulation 7 require service 
providers to communicate to consumers, through SMS as well as email the details of the action taken 
on the complaint together with the procedure for preferring an appeal before the Appellate Authority, 
their appellate rights and the applicable timelines. 


With respect to stakeholders’ suggestions regarding complaint registration through various channels, 
including ‘in-person’, it is noted that the proposed amendments to regulation 9 additionally expand 
the modes through which appeals may be filed, including email, mobile application, website/portal, 
Complaint Centre, post, and in-person submission, thereby enhancing accessibility and convenience 
for consumers. Complaints and appeals may also be lodged in person, as already provided under 
regulation 3(5), and regulation 9(2). 


With respect to stakeholders’ concerns regarding consumers who may be unable to file an appeal 
within the prescribed period of fifteen days and the consequent suggestions for extending the appeal 
filing period, the Authority clarifies that, while an appeal is ordinarily required to be filed within 
fifteen (5) days, as per sub-regulation (3), the Appellate Authority may entertain an appeal filed after 
the expiry of the prescribed period but before the lapse of three (3) months from the expiry of the time 
limit specified in regulation 8, provided it is satisfied that sufficient cause existed for the delay, 


92 


THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : EXTRAORDINARY [PART III—SEc.4] 


VII. 


IX. 


thereby ensuring that consumers are not deprived of their right to appeal on account of unforeseen 
circumstances or genuine difficulties in filing the appeal within the stipulated period. 


The amendment is designed to facilitate timely escalation of unresolved grievances and to support a 
more streamlined and efficient appeal resolution framework. 


In order to strengthen the regulatory framework, it is imperative that the Appellate Authority 
established by the Service Providers in pursuance of this regulation shall also register and resolve the 
appeals made for complaints regarding receipt of unsolicited commercial communications (UCC). 
Therefore, the regulation has been amended to mandate that the Appellate Authority shall resolve and 
reply to such appeals in a manner and within a period as prescribed under this regulation. 


Regulation 0 - Appellate Authority - Establishment and Composition: 

Sub-regulation (), (2), (3) and (5) 

28) Composition of appellate authority, dissemination of information and placement of quarterly report 
before Board/CEO of the company. 


a) Inputs of the Stakeholders: 


I. 


Il. 


Il. 


IV. 


VI. 


VII. 


VII. 


Some stakeholders supported requiring senior management officials as Appellate Authorities. 


Some stakeholders opposed prescribing the composition of the Appellate Authority, including seniority, 
experience and organisational structure, contending that such matters should remain within the 
operational discretion of service providers and that the proposed requirements amount to micro- 
regulation without demonstrable consumer benefit. It was argued that the five-year experience 
requirement for appointment to Appellate Authority is unduly restrictive and should be left to 
operational discretion of the Service Providers. 


Some stakeholders opposed the requirement of establishing a separate Appellate Authority for each 
Licensed Service Area (LSA), contending that centralised appellate mechanisms already function 
effectively and that LSA-wise authorities would create unnecessary administrative burden and 
duplication. 


Some stakeholders opposed the requirement to publish Appellate Authority details in newspapers on the 
ground that digital platforms provide real-time, updated and more effective dissemination of 
information. 


Some stakeholders also described the mandatory Board/CEO reporting requirement as micro-regulation 
of corporate governance. 


One stakeholder advocated an independent authority with participation of recognised Voluntary 
Consumer Organisations/ Consumer Advocacy Groups. It was contended that the existing three-tier 
grievance redressal mechanism lacks independence and advocated an external adjudicatory authority 
empowered to award compensation. They expressed concerns regarding potential institutional bias 
arising from appointing senior management employees as Appellate Authorities and proposed 
prescribing fit-and-proper criteria, conflict-of-interest safeguards, recusal provisions and reasoned 
written orders. 


Some stakeholders highlighted the requirement of an Appellate Authority in every LSA and suggested 
expanding the Appellate Authority to a 2-member body with one senior member from the Service 
Provider with 5 years of experience and the other from Consumer Advocacy Group while some others 
recommended an odd-numbered or multi-membered body so decisions can be reached by majority. 


Some stakeholders asked to retain external consumer participation through the Advisory Committee to 
ensure balanced decision-making. They also contended that complaints should be taken as feedback. 


[भाग ॥|--खण्ड 4] भारत का राजपत्र : असाधारण 93 


IX. 


Some stakeholders recommended establishing an independent Telecom Consumer Ombudsman as a 
second-level external review mechanism to complement the existing grievance redressal framework and 
strengthen transparency, accountability and consumer confidence. 


b) Analysis: 


I. 


Il. 


Il. 


IV. 


VI. 


VII. 


VII. 


IX. 


The Authority has examined the stakeholder comments regarding the establishment and composition 
of the Appellate Authority, demanding operational discretion of service providers in determining the 
organisational structure and highlighting that reporting to CEO/Board of Directors amounts to micro- 
regulation of corporate governance. 


The proposed provisions are not prescriptive in nature and do not amount to micro-regulation of the 
internal organizational structures of service providers. Rather, the proposed framework seeks to lay 
down broad principles and minimum governance standards necessary for ensuring an effective, fair 
and consumer-centric appellate mechanism. 


No requirement has been introduced for submission of signed or authorised proofs, records 
evidencing steps taken, or action taken reports from the Chief Executive Officer. The requirement is 
intended solely to ensure that periodic reports relating to complaints, appeals and the results of online 
consumer surveys are placed before the Chief Executive Officer or the Board of Directors, as the case 
may be, for their perusal and review, thereby enabling timely identification of systemic bottlenecks 
and appropriate managerial oversight at a senior level. 


The Authority is of the view that an individual possessing adequate seniority, competence and 
relevant experience is better placed to independently assess grievances and render informed decisions 
on appeals. At the same time, the proposed framework provides sufficient flexibility to service 
providers in determining the precise organizational arrangements for implementation. 


Further, the Authority has not proposed any requirement for prior approval, nomination or vetting of 
such personnel by the Authority. The amendments merely establish broad parameters intended to 
strengthen accountability, independence and consumer confidence in the appellate grievance redressal 
process while allowing service providers adequate operational flexibility. 


Some stakeholders have stated that establishing a separate Appellate Authority for each Licensed 
Service Area (LSA) would amount to an unnecessary administrative burden. The Authority notes that 
this is not a change from the existing regulation and continuing with a separate Appellate Authority at 
each LSA will benefit both the consumers and service providers as the redressal of the appeals would 
be more efficient. Amendment also provides that a service provider having all India license or 
authorization, may, for the purpose of these regulations, establish one or more Appellate Authorities 
for its service areas. 


Some stakeholders suggested expanding the Appellate Authority to a two-member body with one 
senior member from the Service Provider with five years of experience and the other from Consumer 
Advocacy Group while some others recommended an odd-numbered or multi-membered body so 
decisions can be reached by majority. Authority has considered the matter and notes that the primary 
responsibility of the redressal of the complaint and appeals lies with the service providers, and 
inclusion of the other members may lead to dilution of responsibility and will delay the process, hence 
the amendment will make the process of redressal of appeals faster. 


With respect to stakeholders' comments regarding the requirement for newspaper publication, the 
Authority reiterates its position as set out under regulation 5()(a). 


With respect to suggestions for appointment of an adjudicatory or an independent authority with 
participation of recognised Voluntary Consumer Organisations/Consumer Advocacy Groups, the 
following is relevant: 


i. To establish an effective mechanism for consumer redressal, TRAI had sent its 
“Recommendations on Complaint/ Grievance Redressal in the Telecom Sector”, inter-alia, 


94 


THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : EXTRAORDINARY [PART III—SEc.4] 


recommending setting up of office of Telecom Ombudsman to Department of 
Telecommunication (DoT) on 0.03.207. 


il. However, taking into account the availability of a quasi-judicial mechanism for handling of 
consumer complaints and national-wide infrastructure to provide comprehensive redressal 
under a law in the Consumer Protection Act, 209, the DoT, informed to TRAI that another 
independent structure for the resolution of grievances of telecom consumers which inter-alia 
includes Telecom Ombudsman is not required. 


There have been concerns regarding potential institutional bias arising from appointing senior 
management employees as Appellate Authorities. The Authority has proposed a Consumer Grievance 
Redressal Oversight Committee under regulation Il to periodically participate and suggest 
recommendations in consumers’ interest. 


Regulation ] Consumer Grievance Redressal Oversight Committee Establishment, Composition and Function: 


29) Removal of Advisory committee to the Appellate Authority. 
a) Inputs of the Stakeholders: 


I. 


Il. 


Il. 


IV. 


VI. 


VII. 


Some stakeholders supported requiring senior management officials as Appellate Authorities. Certain 
Service Providers have also commended the proposed amendment. 


Several stakeholders opposed the proposed removal of the Advisory Committee and Consumer 
Advocacy Groups (CAGs), contending that the existing mechanism provides independent oversight, 
transparency, accountability and consumer confidence, and that its removal would leave grievance 
redressal entirely under the control of Telecom Service Providers (TSPs), resulting in institutional bias 
and weakening procedural fairness. They further disputed the Authority's observation that the Advisory 
Committee caused delays, asserting that delays primarily resulted from Telecom Service Providers 
implementation practices rather than the Advisory Committee itself. They raised concerns regarding 
institutional neutrality and advocated for reformation rather than removal such as through stricter 5-7 
days turnaround timelines. 


Some stakeholders submitted that, instead of abolishing the Advisory Committee, its functioning should 
be strengthened through clearly defined SOPs, virtual meetings, fixed timelines, digital monitoring, 
periodic audits and enhanced accountability. They recommended mandating Advisory Committee 
review to be conducted via digital/virtual hearings within 7-l0 days window; otherwise the Appellate 
Authority may proceed and make it legally binding for Advisory Committee to meet on every 4", 20, 
20" and 28" of every month and imposition of Financial Disincentives upon non-compliance. 


It was suggested that at the TRAI level, provision should be made for a Lokpal arrangement for 
resolution of consumer problems. Stakeholders also suggested TRAI to mandate an independent audit of 
a random sample of dismissed appeals by panel of TRAI registered Consumer Advocacy Group 
members. 


Some suggested the establishment of an independent Telecom Ombudsman, external appellate 
mechanism, regional appellate benches or independent review mechanism to ensure impartiality in 
grievance redressal. 


Other stakeholders recommended additional safeguards such as independent monitoring of complaint 
handling systems, regular Open House discussions, publication of appellate orders and grievance 
statistics, trackable acknowledgements, multilingual complaint systems and improved accessibility for 
consumers. 


Some stakeholders suggested that a Consumer Helpline similar to the National Consumer Helpline 
(NCH) should be independently monitored and not solely controlled by TSPs. Some recommend 
inclusion of Consumer Advocacy Group within the Appellate Authority, headed by an independent 
nominee instead of a representative of the concerned company. 


[भाग ॥|--खण्ड 4] भारत का राजपत्र : असाधारण 95 


VII. 


Another stakeholder stated that the appeal system becomes operator reviewing operator” and is against 
the principle of natural justice. When we compare with the situation in other jurisdictions across the 
world, we find that the consumers in India are disadvantaged. UK uses independent Alternate Dispute 
Resolution bodies, Australia has an independent Telecommunications Industry Ombudsman and EU has 
independent dispute resolution mechanisms. India currently lacks a truly independent telecom 
ombudsman. Hence, it is imperative that the Appellate Advisory Committee be retained with 
modifications. 


b) Analysis: 


I. 


Il. 


Il. 


IV. 


VI. 


VIL. 


VII. 


The Authority noted the concerns expressed by stakeholders regarding the role historically envisaged for 
Advisory Committees as an additional layer of consumer representation in the grievance redressal 
framework. However, a review of the implementation of the existing framework indicates that the 
Advisory Committee mechanism has not functioned uniformly across Licensed Service Areas (LSAs) and 
has not consistently achieved the intended objective of providing timely and effective consumer 
participation in the appellate process. 


It is noted that meetings of Advisory Committees were often conducted irregularly and their functioning 
was largely dependent upon the availability of external members. In several cases, scheduling constraints, 
logistical challenges and non-availability of members resulted in delays in the disposal of appeals. 


These challenges were particularly pronounced in LSAs where participation of Consumer Advocacy 
Group representatives frequently required substantial travel and coordination efforts, making regular 
meetings difficult to organise. Consequently, the Advisory Committee mechanism often contributed to 
procedural delays rather than facilitating expeditious grievance resolution. 


With respect to concerns regarding institutional neutrality and weakening of procedural fairness, the 
Authority is of the view that consumer grievance redressal mechanisms should be guided by the 
principles of accountability, transparency and timeliness. Under the existing framework, responsibility for 
delays in appeal disposal could become diffused between the service provider, the Appellate Authority 
and the Advisory Committee process. The proposed amendment seeks to establish clear institutional 
accountability by placing the responsibility for appeal handling and timely disposal squarely upon the 
service provider and its designated Appellate Authority. 


Further, addressing the concerns regarding conflict of interest, the Authority notes that while complaint 
resolution is a responsibility of the Service Providers, their grievance redressal systems are subject to 
consistent regulatory oversight, thereby preventing unilateral or arbitrary decision-making by Service 
Providers. The proposed amendment cites various mechanisms to highlight any inadequacies in complaint 
handling and hence, further boost public confidence. The original framework itself envisaged 
representation from the service provider. Further, the advice rendered by the Advisory Committee was 
recommendatory in nature and not binding upon the Appellate Authority, which retained the final 
decision-making responsibility. 


With respect to suggestions for independent audits to be conducted by the Authority and reports being 
given to registered Consumer Advocacy Groups for review, the Authority notes that the proposed 
amendments already prescribe mechanisms of Audit and Inspection (regulation 8) and Regulatory 
review (regulation 8A). Further, Service Providers are required to publish performance reports in public 
domain under regulation L5. 


The Authority has carefully considered the stakeholder suggestions aimed at strengthening the Advisory 
Committee framework and imposition of financial disincentives upon non-compliance. It is prescribing 
detailed procedures governing the constitution and functioning of such committees would encroach upon 
the operational autonomy of service providers and render the framework unduly prescriptive. Further, 
imposition of financial disincentives for irregular conduct of meetings may not be appropriate, as such 
matters relate to the internal governance mechanisms of service providers. 


Moreover, the Authority decided that the Advisory Committee shall be replaced by a Consumer 
Grievance Redressal Oversight Committee (serving as an oversight and review mechanism). The 


96 


THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : EXTRAORDINARY [PART III—SEc.4] 


IX. 


XI. 


XI. 


XII. 


XIV. 


XV. 


XVI. 


XVII. 


Consumer Grievance Redressal Oversight Committee shall consist of at least one representative from 
each of the consumer organisation registered with the Authority in the State or Union Territory of the 
licensed service area in which the subscriber base of the service provider is above ten thousand and 
representatives of the Service Provider. 


The Consumer Grievance Redressal Oversight Committee shall not examine or advise on individual 
appeal cases. Instead, it shall be apprised, at least once in a financial year, of the overall functioning of the 
appellate mechanism to facilitate institutional oversight without exercising adjudicatory or decision- 
making functions. 


Following the reorganization of the Advisory Committee, service providers would no longer be able to 
attribute delays in appeal disposal to difficulties associated with convening meetings, obtaining quorum, 
or securing participation of external members. Consequently, service providers can be held directly 
accountable for compliance with prescribed timelines and grievance redressal obligations under the 
regulations. 


With regard to stakeholders’ concerns about diminished consumer representation, the Authority notes that 
the proposed amendment does not diminish the importance of consumer feedback or participation in the 
regulatory process. The amended framework introduces and strengthens alternative mechanisms through 
which consumer experiences and concerns may be captured and analysed. These include online consumer 
satisfaction surveys (regulation !4A), enhanced complaint monitoring systems, periodic reporting 
requirements, regulatory review under regulation I8A, performance reviews and direct complaint 
channels available to consumers. 


Therefore, the proposed restructuring of the Advisory Committee framework constitutes a rationalisation 
of the grievance redressal mechanism aimed at reducing procedural delays, enhancing service provider 
accountability and improving the efficiency of appeal resolution. The proposal should accordingly be 
viewed in conjunction with the broader set of measures introduced under these regulations to strengthen 
the overall consumer grievance redressal framework. 


The Authority has also examined stakeholder proposals for creation of an Ombudsman-like mechanism or 
Lokpal or independent consumer grievance adjudication framework within the regulatory architecture at 
TRAI’s level. In this regard, the Authority notes that the Telecom Regulatory Authority of India Act, 
997, as amended by the TRAI (Amendment) Act, 2000, established a clear institutional separation 
between regulatory functions and adjudicatory functions in the telecommunications sector. Following the 
amendment, TRAI was entrusted with recommendatory, regulatory, supervisory and monitoring functions, 
including framing regulations, prescribing quality of service standards, protecting consumer interests and 
ensuring compliance by service providers. Simultaneously, the Telecom Disputes Settlement and 
Appellate Tribunal (TDSAT) was established as the specialised adjudicatory body for resolution of 
disputes and appeals arising within the telecommunications sector. 


Accordingly, the grievance redressal mechanism established under these regulations is intended to 
function as an internal consumer complaint resolution framework of the service provider and not as an 
adjudicatory forum. 


The Authority has reviewed the suggestions for additional safeguards and observes that adequate 
measures have been proposed for strengthening the grievance redressal system. 


With respect to suggestion for a separate helpline by TRAI like NCH, the Authority is of the view that 
NCH already deals with various kinds of consumer complaints, including telecom related complaints. 
Also, a robust system of grievance redressal is most important for consumers as they will get directly 
benefitted by such system. 


Regarding suggestions for TRAI to establish or function as an Ombudsman, the Authority refers to earlier 
paragraph number 28 (b) (IX). 


[भाग ॥|--खण्ड 4] भारत का राजपत्र : असाधारण 97

demonstrated need to mandate a separate office, secretariat, supporting staff or office accommodation 
for the Appellate Authority. 


They contended that consumer interests are adequately safeguarded as long as appeals remain 
accessible, are duly examined and resolved within the prescribed timelines.

Il.

The proposed office along with accommodation and supporting staff is necessary for maintaining

Further, the Authority has not prescribed the number of supporting staff members and thus the 
proposed amendments do not amount to structural prescription.
