Regulation 4 — Registration and Disposal of appeals by Appellate Authority: 
Sub-regulation (), (2) and (3) 
32) ‘Timeline of Registration and Disposal of appeal by Appellate Authority 


a) 


Inputs of the Stakeholders: 


I. 


Il. 


Il. 


Some stakeholders opposed the reduction to I5 days and requested retention of the 30-day timeline, 
citing the need for detailed examination of appeals, verification of records, consultations across 
multiple divisions, and, in some cases, coordination with external agencies and statutory authorities 
while some stakeholders supported the amendment. 


Some stakeholders suggested that the proposed 5-day timeline for disposal of appeals be increased to 
20 days or that Appellate Committee meetings be conducted offline, as committee-based decision- 
making may not be feasible within the shortened timeframe. 


Some supported retaining the existing 30-day timeline, stating that many appeals require deep 
investigation and retrieval of old records, while others recommended further reducing the timeline to 
0 days, with mandatory communication to consumers within five days in case of delay and faster 
processing of refunds. 


98 


THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : EXTRAORDINARY [PART III—SEc.4] 


IV. 


VI. 


One stakeholder proposed introducing automatic financial disincentives for delays beyond I5 days 
and escalation to TRAI for unresolved appeals. 


Another stakeholder emphasised that the regulation should explicitly preserve consumers’ procedural 
rights, including submission of evidence, acknowledgment, hearings, status updates, and reasoned 
written orders, and ensure that appeals are not rejected on mere technical or procedural grounds. 


Some stakeholders urged TRAI to establish a transparent grievance redressal system with complete 
consumer communication and record-keeping. They recommend a reasoned written order to every 
complaint and a transparent, documented appellate process with written, reasoned and signed 
decisions communicated to the consumer. 


b) Analysis: 


I. 


Il. 


Ii. 


IV. 


VI. 


VII. 


VII. 


With regard to stakeholders’ suggestions for increasing the timeline to 20 or 30 days, the Authority notes 
that the primary objective of the proposed amendment is to strengthen the effectiveness of the appellate 
grievance redressal mechanism by ensuring expeditious disposal of consumer appeals. Further, the 
existing regulatory framework was formulated when the appellate process involved participation of the 
Advisory Committee and relied substantially on physical meetings and manual processes. 


Under such circumstances, longer timelines were considered necessary to accommodate scheduling and 
coordination requirements. However, technological advancements and widespread adoption of digital 
communication platforms have significantly transformed grievance handling processes. 


With respect to suggestions for reasoned written orders, the Authority observed that appeals can now be 
received, processed, examined and disposed of through electronic means, including websites, mobile 
applications, e-mail and other digital interfaces. Service providers are required to communicate the action 
taken within three days of the disposal to the appellant through email and SMS and also update the same 
on the website and mobile app. 


It is also noted that under the proposed amendments, the Advisory Committee mechanism has been 
discontinued. Consequently, the Appellate Authority is no longer required to convene meetings, coordinate 
with external members, or await recommendations from the Advisory Committee before taking a final 
decision. The removal of these procedural layers is expected to substantially reduce processing time and 
enable faster disposal of appeals. 


In view of the simplified appellate framework and the increased use of digital processes, the Authority is 
of the opinion that a [5-day timeline for disposal of appeals is both reasonable and achievable. Extending 
the timeline would dilute the objective of ensuring prompt redressal of consumer grievances without any 
corresponding operational necessity. 


With regard to suggestions for offline meetings of Appellate Authority, the Authority observes that such 
matters should be left to Appellate Authority’s discretion. 


Regarding suggestions seeking automatic financial disincentives for every instance of delay, the Authority 
notes that the failure to comply with prescribed timelines, or systemic deficiencies in grievance handling 
can be identified through regulatory oversight mechanisms and addressed through appropriate regulatory 
interventions, including financial disincentives wherever warranted under the applicable provisions of the 
regulations. Moreover, financial disincentives are not imposed automatically, and appropriate opportunity 
is provided to Service Providers to explain their case. 


Regarding suggestions for automatic escalation of unresolved appeals to the Authority, the TRAI Act, 
997 establishes a clear distinction between regulatory functions and adjudicatory functions. The 
Authority's role is to regulate, monitor compliance and protect consumer interests through systemic 
oversight. 


[भाग ॥|--खण्ड 4] भारत का राजपत्र : असाधारण 99 


IX. 


The Authority is not empowered to adjudicate individual consumer disputes or function as an appellate 
forum for individual grievances. Accordingly, automatic escalation of unresolved appeals to the Authority 
would not be consistent with the statutory framework governing telecommunications regulation. 

With respect to concerns highlighting preservation of consumers’ procedural rights, the proposed 
amendments introduce a broader compliance monitoring framework through periodic reporting, consumer 
satisfaction surveys, complaint monitoring systems, audits and regulatory reviews. 


Regulation 4A - Online Consumer Feedback Survey: 
33) Online Consumer Feedback Survey after resolution of complaints and appeals. 


a) Inputs of the Stakeholders:

VIL. 


Some stakeholders supported the proposal, stating that it would shift complaint handling from a 
perfunctory approach to actual consumer satisfaction, improve TSP accountability, and enable outcome- 
based evaluation. 


Some stakeholders opposed Regulation 4.A and requested deletion of the requirement to administer 
Online Consumer Feedback Surveys and share detailed consumer satisfaction data with the QPR 
(Quarterly Performance Report), contending that consumer feedback is already an integral and well- 
established part of the existing complaint handling mechanism, while the proposal would introduce 
significant procedural duplication and compliance overhead. They argued that survey data is primarily an 
internal tool for service enhancement, and publication of granular survey data without adequate 
operational context could lead to misinterpretation. Section l.l(b)(v) of the TRAI Act, 997 requires 
TRAI to carry out periodic surveys of services provided by service providers but does not empower TRAI 
to mandate TSPs to carry out surveys of their own services and publish the results. 


The stakeholders stated that in many cases, complaints relate to non-telecom matters (tower rent, radiation 
concerns, tower fraud, CDR requests, numbers suspended by law enforcement agencies, etc.) where

Some stakeholders stated that survey links be transmitted through a TRAI-operated or independently 
verified platform, disclosure of survey response rates in the Quarterly Performance Reports (QPR), 
establishment of minimum acceptable satisfaction thresholds to trigger regulatory scrutiny, and 
exploration of lightweight incentives to improve participation. 

Some stakeholders also stated that surveys be conducted in Hindi, English, and official languages and be 
accessible through SMS, mobile apps, websites, IVR, and email for maximum inclusivity; participation 
remain entirely voluntary without influence or manipulation; publication of total complaints closed,

implementation framework be developed in consultation with consumer organisations and CAG

independent third-party agency appointed by TRAI based on a well-defined and pre-published 


00 THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : EXTRAORDINARY [PART III—SEc.4] 


methodology. One of the suggestions was for publication of survey reports in public domain. Concerns 
were expressed that online surveys may underrepresent vulnerable consumers and recommended 
independent oversight. 


b) Analysis: 


I. Regarding concerns relating to procedural duplication and compliance burden, it is noted that certain service 
providers agree that they already conduct consumer Feedback surveys. The proposed requirement only seeks 
publication of such Feedback survey outcomes to enhance transparency and strengthen consumer awareness. 
Furthermore, consumer satisfaction surveys are a well-established practice across various service sectors. 


II. With regard to comments on a need to establish adequate operational context, it is reiterated that the purpose of 
the survey mechanism is to identify trends, patterns and areas requiring improvement rather than to serve as 
an isolated compliance parameter. 


III. With respect to suggestions for minimum acceptable satisfaction thresholds, the framework already requires 
service providers to submit adequate consumer Feedback data as part of the Quarterly Performance Reports. 
The Authority, if required, may seek additional information from time to time. It is also noted that consumer 
satisfaction levels on Grievance redressal may vary significantly depending upon the nature of complaints, 
geographical conditions, network characteristics and consumer expectations. Consequently, a single uniform 
satisfaction threshold may not accurately reflect service provider performance across all circumstances. 


IV. Further, the broader amendments proposed under these regulations seek to enhance multilingual accessibility 
across consumer-facing grievance redressal mechanisms. Service providers are therefore expected to ensure 
that consumer interaction channels, including feedback mechanisms, remain accessible and user-friendly for 
consumers across different linguistic backgrounds. Also, the prescribed survey is objective, simple to 
administer and comprehend, and reflects a practice that is widely adopted across service industries. 


V. With respect to stakeholders’ suggestions regarding the methodology for conducting Feedback surveys or 
appointment of third-party agency, the Authority is of the view that prescribing such operational aspects 
would amount to regulatory overreach and unnecessary micro-regulation. Accordingly, the methodology 
shall remain within the operational discretion of service providers. 


VI. Some stakeholders have highlighted that Section Il.l(b)(v) of the TRAI Act, 997 requires TRAI to carry out 
periodic surveys of services provided by service providers but does not empower TRAI to mandate TSPs to 
carry out surveys of their own services and publish the results. In this regard, it is noted that the provision 
under Section I!.I(b)(v) of the TRAI Act, 997 is specific to the survey about the quality of service being 
provided by the service providers, whereas the survey prescribed under regulation 4A is a limited feedback 
mechanism forming integral part of the grievance redressal framework. The Authority's mandate to 
independently conduct surveys under the Act is distinct and remains unaffected by the proposed 
requirement. 


VII. The proposed Feedback survey framework aims to systematically capture consumer feedback immediately 
after closure of complaints and appeals and to provide the Authority with measurable indicators regarding 
the effectiveness of grievance redressal mechanisms. Since the survey is directly linked to individual 
complaints and appeals handled by the concerned service provider, the service provider is best positioned to 
administer the survey contemporaneously with the closure of the grievance.

b) Analysis: 


I. 


Il. 


Ii. 


IV. 


With regard to suggestions for requirement of adequate operational context to avoid misinterpretation of 
data, unlike the existing regulation, which primarily focuses on appeals-related reporting, the proposed 
amendment mandates comprehensive reporting for both complaints and appeals separately for each 
Licensed Service Area (LSA). 


Further, the proposed Quarterly Performance Reports (QPRs) require disclosure of a wide range of 
performance indicators, including complaints and appeals received, disposed of and pending; compliance 
with prescribed timelines, average resolution time, percentage of complaints resolved, consumer 
satisfaction levels and survey results. Such disclosures are intended to provide consumers, service 
providers and TRAI with a more comprehensive understanding of grievance redressal performance. The 
reporting requirements are fundamental, not granular, and are indispensable for ensuring transparency and 
regulatory oversight. 


With regard to suggestions to provide adequate implementation period, the Authority notes that the 
amendment provides a period of sixty days from the date of notification of these regulations for collection 
of primary data, its storage, processing, performance report generation and their online submission to the 
Authority 


Concerns have also been raised for providing flexibility in data retention periods. The authority notes that 
a minimum retention period of six months is necessary to facilitate audits and inspections. Accordingly, to 
strike a balance between data adequacy and compliance burden, the Authority maintains that the six- 
month period is appropriate.

VI. 


VII. 


VII. 


IX. 


Suggestions for dispensing with requirement for publication of Customer Care Number, General 
Information Number etc. have been dealt with under Regulation 5(). 


With respect to the suggestion for a common platform jointly accessible by TSPs, CAGs and TRAI for 
independent verification of grievance resolution, the proposal of Grievance Redressal Oversight 
Committee under Regulation | adequately addresses this aspect. 


With regards to suggestions for introduction of standardised disclosures, the proposed amendment already 
requires service providers to publish comprehensive performance reports on their websites, portals and 
applications within fifteen days of the end of every quarter. These reports include adequate complaint and 
appeal statistics, resolution timelines, pendency levels, consumer satisfaction metrics and survey 
outcomes, striking an appropriate balance between transparency and operational feasibility. More frequent 
reporting may increase compliance burden, require continuous validation of large volumes of operational 
data and potentially result in publication of provisional figures that may subsequently require revision. 


Also, Regulation 5(2)(a) and Regulation |5(2)(b) prescribe a non-exhaustive reporting framework and 
specifically provide that the report shall include, the identified Key Performance Indicators. Further, 
Regulation !5(2)(d) empowers the Authority to amend or modify reporting requirements from time to time 
as necessary. 


Therefore, the proposed framework already provides sufficient flexibility to prescribe additional reporting 
parameters in future based on regulatory experience, consumer requirements and market developments, 
without necessitating frequent amendments to the regulations themselves. 


Some stakeholders suggested sharing of reports with CAGs for review. The Authority notes that reports 
will be published in public domain which can be utilised for review. 


Regulation 7 -Telecom Consumer Charter: 
Sub-regulation (I), (2) and (3) 


35) Information in telecom consumer charter. 


a) Inputs of the Stakeholders: 


I. 


It was suggested that the Telecom Consumer Charter be made available through digital channels, such as 
websites and mobile applications, in place of the proposed requirement to maintain physical copies. 


b) Analysis: 


I. 


Il. 


Il. 


IV. 


The Authority has examined the stakeholder suggestions seeking discontinuation of physical copies of 
the Telecom Consumers Charter and limiting its availability exclusively to digital platforms such as 
websites and mobile applications. 


It is noted that the increasing adoption of digital channels for dissemination of consumer information and 
the efforts undertaken by service providers to make consumer-related information readily available 
through their websites, mobile applications and other digital interfaces. However, it is also true that the 
Telecom Consumers Charter serves as a fundamental document informing consumers of their rights, 
obligations, available services, grievance redressal mechanisms and other important service-related 
information. 


Exclusive reliance on digital modes of dissemination may inadvertently exclude certain categories of 
consumers who may have limited access to digital devices, internet connectivity or the requisite digital 
literacy to access such information. This is particularly relevant for consumers residing in rural and 
remote areas, senior citizens, economically disadvantaged consumers and other sections of society that 
may continue to rely upon physical modes of communication. 


Accordingly, the Authority is of the view that retaining physical availability of the Telecom Consumers 
Charter, alongside digital dissemination through websites and mobile applications, is necessary to

V. 


promote inclusivity, reduce information asymmetry and ensure that no category of consumers is 
deprived of access to essential consumer information. 


Also, such physical copies need to be provided only once during the time of subscription for service. 


Regulation 8- Inspection and auditing, 

Regulation 8A - Regulatory review, 

Regulation 8B - Consequences for contravention of the provisions of the regulations, 
Regulation 8C - Payment of financial disincentive: 


36) Provisions of Audit, regulatory review, Consequences for non-compliances and payment of financial 
disincentives. 


a) Inputs of the Stakeholders: 


I. 


Il. 


Il. 


IV. 


VI. 


Some stakeholders supported the introduction of financial disincentives (FDs) in principle as a measure 
to strengthen regulatory compliance and accountability, considering them a necessary and proportionate 
mechanism for ensuring compliance and enhancing accountability, given the inherent imbalance in 
bargaining powers between individual consumers and large service providers. They advocated that QoS 
parameters measure macro-level network uptime, whereas this regulation addresses individual consumer 
grievance mismanagement - so there is no double jeopardy. 


Some stakeholders urged the deletion of the proposed financial disincentive framework for improper 
disposal of complaints/appeals and delayed submission of Quarterly Performance Reports (QPRs), 
expressing concerns regarding its legal basis, proportionality, and regulatory approach. They further 
argued upon imposition of double penalties on TSPs, given the existence of Financial Disincentives 
under QoS Regulations and opposed introduction of FDs as unnecessary financial and operational 
burden. 


Some stakeholders suggested to review the proposed mandatory one-year record retention requirement 
for complaint and appeal records by prescribing a shorter retention period or providing greater 
operational flexibility, such as aligning complaint and appeal record retention requirements with 
regulation 7, contending that retention beyond three months after disposal is disproportionate and offers 
limited additional consumer benefit. 


Some stakeholders also recommended strengthening the compliance and enforcement framework 
through independent audits, periodic review of complaint handling practices, enhanced monitoring 
mechanisms, greater involvement of Consumer Advocacy Groups (CAGs), and increased transparency 
through regular public engagement and publishing periodic independent audit reports on TRAI website. 


Some stakeholders suggested linking penalties to factors such as the scale of violations, number of 
affected consumers, repeat instances of non-compliance, or revenue, so as to enhance their deterrent 
effect. It was also suggested that appropriate consumer compensation may be considered in cases where 
penalties are imposed. 


Other stakeholders suggested reviewing the proposed financial disincentive framework by introducing 
objective and clearly defined criteria for imposing penalties and adopting a graded or threshold-based 
approach linked to systemic non-compliance rather than individual complaint-level assessments while 
demanding greater clarity in the provisions relating to ‘improper dismissal/disposal' by defining the term 
explicitly, specifying illustrative circumstances constituting improper disposal, and introducing 
safeguards such as the right to seek reopening of improperly closed complaints. 


b) Analysis: 


I. 


It is noted that improper disposal is related to non-compliance of the provisions of the regulation, i.e., the 
complaint or appeal was closed without following the prescribed process, timelines or mandatory 
obligations etc.. 


04 THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : EXTRAORDINARY [PART III—SEc.4] 
Il. Without prejudice to the generality of the foregoing, improper disposal shall include the following 
instances of non-compliance: 
i. failure to register a complaint immediately upon its receipt and assign a unique docket number 
under regulation 7(); 
il. failure to communicate to the consumer, through the prescribed channels, the status of the 
complaint, actions taken, anticipated resolution timeline, reasons for delay, or the final decision, 
wherever required under these regulations under regulation 6() (b); 
lil. failure to communicate to the consumer the action taken on the complaint along with the 
procedure for preferring an appeal to the Appellate Authority under regulation 7(3); 
iv. failure to dispose of complaints or appeals within the timelines prescribed under these regulations 
without recording and communicating reasons for delay, wherever required, under regulation 8; 
v. failure to acknowledge an appeal immediately upon its receipt by assigning and communicating a 
unique appeal number through the prescribed modes under regulation !4(); 
Vi. failure to communicate the decision of the Appellate Authority within the prescribed time and 
update the same on the website, mobile application or other prescribed platforms under regulation 
4(3); 
Vii. failure to administer the prescribed online complaint/appeal Feedback Survey upon closure or 
disposal of a complaint or appeal under regulation I4A. 

Il. Some stakeholders questioned the legality of FDs, contending that the TRAI Act, 997 does not empower the 
Authority to impose financial disincentives through delegated legislation. In this regard, reference is drawn 
towards the TRAI Act, !997 which confers power on the Authority not only to regulate but also to ensure the 
compliance of the provisions of the regulations and Tariff Orders. The word “ensure” has a mandatory 
connotation, it means “make certain”. Furthermore, the Hon’ble Supreme Court, in its judgment dated the 7, 
August 2007, in Civil Appeal No. 2!04/2006 (Central Power Distribution Co. & Ors Vs. CERC & Anr), inter 
alia, held that “it is well settled that a power to regulate includes within it power to enforce”. The main 
purposes for enactment of the TRAI Act, !997, as discernible from the preamble thereof, and as applicable to 
TRAIT are: 

i. to regulate telecommunication services; 
il. to protect the interest of the service providers and consumers of telecommunication services; and 
lil. to ensure orderly growth of telecom sector. 
A reading of the various provisions of TRAI Act, 997 makes it clear that the protection of the interest of the 
consumers has been given paramount importance by the legislature. 
IV. Apart from casting duty on the Authority to protect the interest of the consumer, TRAI Act, 997 under section 


l(/)(d) confers power on TRAI to perform such other functions including such administrative and financial 
functions as may be necessary to carry out the provisions of the Act. There are a catena of judgments by the 
Supreme Court wherein the Hon’ble Court has repeatedly re-stated the proposition that legislation should be 
read and interpreted so as to further the purpose of its enactment and not in a manner that derogates from its 
main objectives. The Hon’ble Supreme Court in its judgment in the case of State of Karnataka Vs. 
Vishwabharthi House Building Co-operative Societies and Ors. [(2004) 5 SCC 430], has referred the judgment 
of Hon’ble Guwahati High Court in the case of Arabind Das Vs. State of Assam & Ors. [AIR 98] Gau 8 
(FB)] wherein it was, inter alia, held that where a statute gives a power, such power implies that legitimate 
steps may be taken to exercise that power even though these steps may not be clearly spelt out in the statute. 
The Hon’ble Court further held that in determining whether a power claimed by a statutory authority can be 
held to be incidental or ancillary to the powers specially conferred by the statute, the court must not only see

whether the power may be derived by reasonable implication from the provisions of the statute, but also 
whether such powers are necessary for carrying out the purposes of the provision of the statute which confers 
power on the Authority in exercise of such powers. 


The Authority notes that the proposed framework does not amount to double jeopardy. The Standards of 
Quality of Service of Access (Wireline and Wireless) and Broadband (Wireline and Wireless) Service 
Regulations, 2024 and Telecom Consumers Complaint Redressal (Fourth Amendment) Regulations, 2026 are 
issued under the Authority's powers conferred by section 36 of the TRAI Act, !997 and serve distinct 
regulatory objectives. The Standards of Quality of Service of Access (Wireline and Wireless) and Broadband 
(Wireline and Wireless) Service Regulations, 2024 address network performance, at the macro level (i.e. LSA 
Level) while Telecom Consumers Complaint Redressal (Fourth Amendment) Regulations, 2026 focuses on 
consumer grievance redressal framework. It is noted that QoS Regulations attract FDs upon failure to submit 
compliance reports under regulation 3 of QoS Regulations and failure to meet benchmarks at LSA level. 
However, the proposed amendment to Telecom Consumers Complaint Redressal (Fourth Amendment) 
Regulations, 2026 attracts FDs upon failure in submission of QPRs as specified under regulation I5 of the 
proposed amendment and cases of non-compliance highlighted during regulatory review. Moreover, the 
Authority does not envisage unilateral imposition of Financial Disincentives (FDs) and shall provide service 
providers with an adequate opportunity to present their case before any such action is taken. 


With regard to demands for shorter retention periods, the Authority notes that a 6- month period shall be 
adequate and in alignment with regulation 7. It notes that a minimum retention period of six months is 
necessary to facilitate audits and inspections. Accordingly, to strike a balance between data adequacy and 
compliance burden, the Authority maintains that the six-month period is appropriate. 


Stakeholders have suggested for increased transparency through greater involvement of Consumer Advocacy 
Groups. It is observed that the proposed amendments adequately address this aspect. 


Stakeholders suggested linking penalties with scale of violations and introduction of graded threshold-based 
approach. It is noted that the proposed criteria of wrongful disposal adequately clarify such grounds for 
penalties. 


Stakeholders suggested consumer compensation for penalties. Such matters amount to dealing with individual 
complaint issues which is not covered its regulatory mandate under TRAI Act, 997.
